Yes. Definitely. I think at first it was mostly the condo thing, or at least that was the thing I could point to and say, “Okay, this is why I’m stressed.” But now it doesn’t stay there at all. It’s like my mind uses that as a starting point and then just jumps to everything else.

Like I’ll worry about whether my cousin is upset with me because she texted a little differently than usual, and then I’ll replay what I said and wonder if I sounded rude or needy or something. Then at work, I’ll worry I’m going to make some stupid mistake on a support call, even if it’s something I technically know how to handle. I’ll be thinking ahead like, what if I misunderstand the issue, what if I give the wrong answer, what if someone complains.

And then it goes bigger, too, like how I’m ever going to save money or move forward with my life. It’s not just one problem anymore. It feels like my brain keeps scanning for things that could go wrong, and there’s always something available.

Definitely. I think at first it was mostly the condo thing, or at least that was the thing I could point to and say, “Okay, this is why I’m stressed.” But now it doesn’t stay there at all. It’s like my mind uses that as a starting point and then just jumps to everything else.\n\nLike I’ll worry about whether my cousin is upset with me because she texted a little differently than usual, and then I’ll replay what I said and wonder if I sounded rude or needy or something. Then at work, I’ll worry I’m going to make some stupid mistake on a support call, even if it’s something I technically know how to handle. I’ll be thinking ahead like, what if I misunderstand the issue, what if I give the wrong answer, what if someone complains.\n\nAnd then it goes bigger, too, like how I’m ever going to save money or move forward with my life. It’s not just one problem anymore. It feels like my brain keeps scanning for things that could go wrong, and there’s always something available.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Most days? I’d say about nine months now. It really started around when the condo loan situation began moving toward foreclosure. Before that I mean, I’d get stressed like anyone, but this ongoing anxious, on-edge feeling… that’s been since then.

It’s like my mind uses that as a starting point and then just jumps to everything else.\n\nLike I’ll worry about whether my cousin is upset with me because she texted a little differently than usual, and then I’ll replay what I said and wonder if I sounded rude or needy or something. Then at work, I’ll worry I’m going to make some stupid mistake on a support call, even if it’s something I technically know how to handle. I’ll be thinking ahead like, what if I misunderstand the issue, what if I give the wrong answer, what if someone complains.\n\nAnd then it goes bigger, too, like how I’m ever going to save money or move forward with my life. It’s not just one problem anymore. It feels like my brain keeps scanning for things that could go wrong, and there’s always something available.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been feeling this anxious and on edge most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I’d say about nine months now. It really started around when the condo loan situation began moving toward foreclosure. Before that I mean, I’d get stressed like anyone, but this ongoing anxious, on-edge feeling… that’s been since then.\n\nIt’s weird because at first I thought, okay, this is just because of the foreclosure stuff, and once I understand what’s happening or once something gets resolved, I’ll calm down. But it didn’t really work that way. It’s been kind of consistently there since then, like in the background most days. I don’t think it would fully go away, honestly. I wish I could say yes, because that would make it feel simpler, like there’s one main problem and once it’s fixed my brain will calm down. And I do think resolving the condo situation would help a lot — like it would take away this huge pressure that’s been hanging over me.

But the worry doesn’t really stay there anymore. It kind of spreads. I’ll start with something related to the condo, and then suddenly I’m thinking, what if my cousin is mad at me because her text sounded a little short, or I’ll replay something I said and wonder if I came off wrong. Then at work I’ll be on a support call and worry I’m going to make a mistake or misunderstand something, even if I know what I’m doing. And then it turns into bigger stuff, like how I’m supposed to save money or move forward with my life at all.

So… no, I don’t think it would mostly disappear. I think one big trigger would be gone, but my mind would probably just find the next thing to latch onto.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… it has. More than I wanted to admit at first.

At work especially, I’ve started falling behind on support tickets because I keep checking and re-checking things that should be simple. Like, I’ll write a response to someone or document a fix, and instead of just sending it or closing the ticket, I’ll read it over again and again because I’m worried I missed something obvious or phrased something wrong. And then something that should take a few minutes turns into this whole drawn-out thing.

It’s gotten to the point where coworkers have had to pick up the slack sometimes, which feels awful, because I don’t want to be that person. I know they’re not necessarily mad at me, but I feel embarrassed, like I’m not carrying my weight.

And outside of work, I’ve skipped several dance nights and mentoring sessions. Those are things I normally care about and actually enjoy, but lately I’ll feel so overwhelmed before going that I just don’t show up. I tell myself I’ll go next time, or that I’m too drained, but then afterward I feel guilty and kind of disappointed in myself. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my day-to-day life.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it has been stronger than I’d expect, even though the foreclosure situation is genuinely stressful. I don’t want to act like that’s a small thing, because it’s not. But the level of worry feels like it’s taken on a life of its own.

At first it made sense to me that I was anxious about the condo, because that was the obvious problem. But now it doesn’t stay there. It jumps around. I’ll be worrying about the condo, and then suddenly I’m wondering if my cousin is upset with me because she answered a text kind of briefly, and I’ll replay the whole exchange in my head. Or I’ll be at work on a support call and start worrying that I’m going to make a mistake or miss something, even when I’ve handled similar things plenty of times before. And then it turns into this bigger spiral about how I’m ever going to save money or get my life moving in the right direction.

So… yes, I think it’s more than what the foreclosure alone explains. It feels like my brain is treating everything like a possible disaster now, not just that one situation.

And then something that should take a few minutes turns into this whole drawn-out thing.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where coworkers have had to pick up the slack sometimes, which feels awful, because I don’t want to be that person. I know they’re not necessarily mad at me, but I feel embarrassed, like I’m not carrying my weight.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped several dance nights and mentoring sessions. Those are things I normally care about and actually enjoy, but lately I’ll feel so overwhelmed before going that I just don’t show up. I tell myself I’ll go next time, or that I’m too drained, but then afterward I feel guilty and kind of disappointed in myself. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety has been stronger than you’d expect for the condo foreclosure situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has been stronger than I’d expect, even though the foreclosure situation is genuinely stressful. I don’t want to act like that’s a small thing, because it’s not. But the level of worry feels like it’s taken on a life of its own.\n\nAt first it made sense to me that I was anxious about the condo, because that was the obvious problem. But now it doesn’t stay there. It jumps around. I’ll be worrying about the condo, and then suddenly I’m wondering if my cousin is upset with me because she answered a text kind of briefly, and I’ll replay the whole exchange in my head. Or I’ll be at work on a support call and start worrying that I’m going to make a mistake or miss something, even when I’ve handled similar things plenty of times before. And then it turns into this bigger spiral about how I’m ever going to save money or get my life moving in the right direction.\n\nSo… yes, I think it’s more than what the foreclosure alone explains. It feels like my brain is treating everything like a possible disaster now, not just that one situation.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find it hard to control or stop the worrying once it starts?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s actually one of the most frustrating parts, because I can recognize, logically, that I’ve already done the thing I’m worried about. Like with work, I’ll tell myself, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You already sent the email, it’s fine,” but that doesn’t really stop it. The thought just keeps looping in the background.

And then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back. What if I missed a number, what if I misunderstood the request, what if the email sounded too blunt, what if someone notices and thinks I’m careless… it just branches out. I try to reassure myself, but it doesn’t always stick, or it only works for a minute and then I’m right back in it.

So yes, I do find it hard to control once it starts. I can sometimes distract myself eventually, but it takes a lot more effort than it feels like it should.

Like, I do get really uncomfortable in my body sometimes, but I don’t know that it’s this sudden surge of intense fear that comes on all at once. It’s a little hard for me to separate everything out, but the timing lines up with when the condo loan started moving toward foreclosure. That was about nine months ago.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just a stressful financial/legal kind of situation and that I was reacting normally, but then the feeling just didn’t really shut off. Like once that process started, it was as if my brain decided everything was unstable and dangerous, and I’ve kind of been stuck in that mode since then. So… yes, I’d say around nine months, connected to the condo loan situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about several different parts of your life, not just the condo situation?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. I think at first it was mostly the condo thing, or at least that was the thing I could point to and say, “Okay, this is why I’m stressed.” But now it doesn’t stay there at all. It’s like my mind uses that as a starting point and then just jumps to everything else.\n\nLike I’ll worry about whether my cousin is upset with me because she texted a little differently than usual, and then I’ll replay what I said and wonder if I sounded rude or needy or something. Then at work, I’ll worry I’m going to make some stupid mistake on a support call, even if it’s something I technically know how to handle. I’ll be thinking ahead like, what if I misunderstand the issue, what if I give the wrong answer, what if someone complains.\n\nAnd then it goes bigger, too, like how I’m ever going to save money or move forward with my life. It’s not just one problem anymore. It feels like my brain keeps scanning for things that could go wrong, and there’s always something available.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been feeling this anxious and on edge most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I’d say about nine months now. It really started around when the condo loan situation began moving toward foreclosure. It kind of spreads. I’ll start with something related to the condo, and then suddenly I’m thinking, what if my cousin is mad at me because her text sounded a little short, or I’ll replay something I said and wonder if I came off wrong. Then at work I’ll be on a support call and worry I’m going to make a mistake or misunderstand something, even if I know what I’m doing. And then it turns into bigger stuff, like how I’m supposed to save money or move forward with my life at all.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think it would mostly disappear. I think one big trigger would be gone, but my mind would probably just find the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Has this anxiety been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve started falling behind on support tickets because I keep checking and re-checking things that should be simple. It kind of spreads. I’ll start with something related to the condo, and then suddenly I’m thinking, what if my cousin is mad at me because her text sounded a little short, or I’ll replay something I said and wonder if I came off wrong. Then at work I’ll be on a support call and worry I’m going to make a mistake or misunderstand something, even if I know what I’m doing. And then it turns into bigger stuff, like how I’m supposed to save money or move forward with my life at all.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think it would mostly disappear. I think one big trigger would be gone, but my mind would probably just find the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Has this anxiety been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve started falling behind on support tickets because I keep checking and re-checking things that should be simple. Like, I’ll write a response to someone or document a fix, and instead of just sending it or closing the ticket, I’ll read it over again and again because I’m worried I missed something obvious or phrased something wrong. And then something that should take a few minutes turns into this whole drawn-out thing.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where coworkers have had to pick up the slack sometimes, which feels awful, because I don’t want to be that person. I know they’re not necessarily mad at me, but I feel embarrassed, like I’m not carrying my weight.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped several dance nights and mentoring sessions. Those are things I normally care about and actually enjoy, but lately I’ll feel so overwhelmed before going that I just don’t show up. I tell myself I’ll go next time, or that I’m too drained, but then afterward I feel guilty and kind of disappointed in myself. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety has been stronger than you’d expect for the condo foreclosure situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has been stronger than I’d expect, even though the foreclosure situation is genuinely stressful. I’ll be worrying about the condo, and then suddenly I’m wondering if my cousin is upset with me because she answered a text kind of briefly, and I’ll replay the whole exchange in my head. Like with work, I’ll tell myself, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You already sent the email, it’s fine,” but that doesn’t really stop it. The thought just keeps looping in the background.\n\nAnd then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back. What if I missed a number, what if I misunderstood the request, what if the email sounded too blunt, what if someone notices and thinks I’m careless… it just branches out. I try to reassure myself, but it doesn’t always stick, or it only works for a minute and then I’m right back in it.\n\nSo yes, I do find it hard to control once it starts. Yes, very often. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because I feel wound up almost all the time.

It’s like my body is bracing for the next bad thing, even when nothing is actively happening. I can be sitting at my desk, just doing normal work stuff, and I’ll notice my shoulders are tight or I’m kind of on edge, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong. And it’s not always dramatic from the outside—I’m not pacing around constantly or anything—but internally I feel keyed up, like I can’t fully settle.

Even at dance nights, which should be fun and something I usually enjoy, I’ll sometimes feel this tension in me, like I’m physically there but my body is still in “watch out” mode. It makes it hard to relax into anything. So yes… restless is a good word for it, but it also feels like being tense and wound tight all the time.

It kind of spreads. Then at work I’ll be on a support call and worry I’m going to make a mistake or misunderstand something, even if I know what I’m doing. And then it turns into bigger stuff, like how I’m supposed to save money or move forward with my life at all.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think it would mostly disappear. I think one big trigger would be gone, but my mind would probably just find the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Has this anxiety been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. Like, I’ll write a response to someone or document a fix, and instead of just sending it or closing the ticket, I’ll read it over again and again because I’m worried I missed something obvious or phrased something wrong. And then something that should take a few minutes turns into this whole drawn-out thing.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where coworkers have had to pick up the slack sometimes, which feels awful, because I don’t want to be that person. Those are things I normally care about and actually enjoy, but lately I’ll feel so overwhelmed before going that I just don’t show up. The thought just keeps looping in the background.\n\nAnd then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back. What if I missed a number, what if I misunderstood the request, what if the email sounded too blunt, what if someone notices and thinks I’m careless… it just branches out. Yes. That’s the part that’s been confusing to me, because I keep trying to tell myself, “Okay, the condo foreclosure is the reason you’re stressed,” and that would make sense if the worry stayed there. But it doesn’t. It’s like once my mind gets going, it just looks for the next possible problem.

I’ll be thinking about the condo, and then suddenly I’m worrying that my cousin is upset with me because she sent a short text or didn’t use an emoji or something tiny like that. And I know how that sounds, like I’m over-reading it, but in the moment it feels really hard not to analyze it. Then later I’ll be on a support call at work and start worrying I’m going to make some mistake, or that I missed something obvious, even if nothing has actually gone wrong.

And then it can turn into this bigger, vague fear about my life overall—like how am I ever going to save money, how am I going to move forward, what if I’m stuck like this. So yes, even when there isn’t one clear thing happening right then, I still feel anxious. It’s like the worry just moves around and attaches itself to whatever it can find.

I’ll be thinking ahead like, what if I misunderstand the issue, what if I give the wrong answer, what if someone complains.\n\nAnd then it goes bigger, too, like how I’m ever going to save money or move forward with my life. It feels like my brain keeps scanning for things that could go wrong, and there’s always something available.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been feeling this anxious and on edge most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I’d say about nine months now. It really started around when the condo loan situation began moving toward foreclosure. Before that I mean, I’d get stressed like anyone, but this ongoing anxious, on-edge feeling… that’s been since then.\n\nIt’s weird because at first I thought, okay, this is just because of the foreclosure stuff, and once I understand what’s happening or once something gets resolved, I’ll calm down. But it didn’t really work that way. It’s been kind of consistently there since then, like in the background most days. So… yeah, about nine months.\n\nDoctor: If the condo foreclosure situation were fully resolved, do you think your anxiety would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would fully go away, honestly. I wish I could say yes, because that would make it feel simpler, like there’s one main problem and once it’s fixed my brain will calm down. And I do think resolving the condo situation would help a lot — like it would take away this huge pressure that’s been hanging over me.\n\nBut the worry doesn’t really stay there anymore. It kind of spreads. I’ll start with something related to the condo, and then suddenly I’m thinking, what if my cousin is mad at me because her text sounded a little short, or I’ll replay something I said and wonder if I came off wrong. Then at work I’ll be on a support call and worry I’m going to make a mistake or misunderstand something, even if I know what I’m doing. And then it turns into bigger stuff, like how I’m supposed to save money or move forward with my life at all.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think it would mostly disappear. I think one big trigger would be gone, but my mind would probably just find the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Has this anxiety been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve started falling behind on support tickets because I keep checking and re-checking things that should be simple. Like, I’ll write a response to someone or document a fix, and instead of just sending it or closing the ticket, I’ll read it over again and again because I’m worried I missed something obvious or phrased something wrong. And then something that should take a few minutes turns into this whole drawn-out thing.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where coworkers have had to pick up the slack sometimes, which feels awful, because I don’t want to be that person. I know they’re not necessarily mad at me, but I feel embarrassed, like I’m not carrying my weight.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped several dance nights and mentoring sessions. Those are things I normally care about and actually enjoy, but lately I’ll feel so overwhelmed before going that I just don’t show up. I tell myself I’ll go next time, or that I’m too drained, but then afterward I feel guilty and kind of disappointed in myself. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety has been stronger than you’d expect for the condo foreclosure situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has been stronger than I’d expect, even though the foreclosure situation is genuinely stressful. I don’t want to act like that’s a small thing, because it’s not. But the level of worry feels like it’s taken on a life of its own.\n\nAt first it made sense to me that I was anxious about the condo, because that was the obvious problem. But now it doesn’t stay there. It jumps around. I’ll be worrying about the condo, and then suddenly I’m wondering if my cousin is upset with me because she answered a text kind of briefly, and I’ll replay the whole exchange in my head. Or I’ll be at work on a support call and start worrying that I’m going to make a mistake or miss something, even when I’ve handled similar things plenty of times before. And then it turns into this bigger spiral about how I’m ever going to save money or get my life moving in the right direction.\n\nSo… yes, I think it’s more than what the foreclosure alone explains. It feels like my brain is treating everything like a possible disaster now, not just that one situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the most frustrating parts, because I can recognize, logically, that I’ve already done the thing I’m worried about. Like with work, I’ll tell myself, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You already sent the email, it’s fine,” but that doesn’t really stop it. The thought just keeps looping in the background.\n\nAnd then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back. What if I missed a number, what if I misunderstood the request, what if the email sounded too blunt, what if someone notices and thinks I’m careless… it just branches out. I try to reassure myself, but it doesn’t always stick, or it only works for a minute and then I’m right back in it.\n\nSo yes, I do find it hard to control once it starts. I can sometimes distract myself eventually, but it takes a lot more effort than it feels like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, very often. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because I feel wound up almost all the time.\n\nIt’s like my body is bracing for the next bad thing, even when nothing is actively happening. I can be sitting at my desk, just doing normal work stuff, and I’ll notice my shoulders are tight or I’m kind of on edge, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong. And it’s not always dramatic from the outside—I’m not pacing around constantly or anything—but internally I feel keyed up, like I can’t fully settle.\n\nEven at dance nights, which should be fun and something I usually enjoy, I’ll sometimes feel this tension in me, like I’m physically there but my body is still in “watch out” mode. It makes it hard to relax into anything. So yes… restless is a good word for it, but it also feels like being tense and wound tight all the time.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the condo foreclosure, do you feel anxious even when nothing in particular seems wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s the part that’s been confusing to me, because I keep trying to tell myself, “Okay, the condo foreclosure is the reason you’re stressed,” and that would make sense if the worry stayed there. But it doesn’t. It’s like once my mind gets going, it just looks for the next possible problem.\n\nI’ll be thinking about the condo, and then suddenly I’m worrying that my cousin is upset with me because she sent a short text or didn’t use an emoji or something tiny like that. And I know how that sounds, like I’m over-reading it, but in the moment it feels really hard not to analyze it. Then later I’ll be on a support call at work and start worrying I’m going to make some mistake, or that I missed something obvious, even if nothing has actually gone wrong.\n\nAnd then it can turn into this bigger, vague fear about my life overall—like how am I ever going to save money, how am I going to move forward, what if I’m stuck like this. So yes, even when there isn’t one clear thing happening right then, I still feel anxious. It’s like the worry just moves around and attaches itself to whatever it can find.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It makes it hard to relax into anything. So yes… restless is a good word for it, but it also feels like being tense and wound tight all the time.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the condo foreclosure, do you feel anxious even when nothing in particular seems wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s the part that’s been confusing to me, because I keep trying to tell myself, “Okay, the condo foreclosure is the reason you’re stressed,” and that would make sense if the worry stayed there. But it doesn’t. It’s like once my mind gets going, it just looks for the next possible problem.\n\nI’ll be thinking about the condo, and then suddenly I’m worrying that my cousin is upset with me because she sent a short text or didn’t use an emoji or something tiny like that. And I know how that sounds, like I’m over-reading it, but in the moment it feels really hard not to analyze it. Then later I’ll be on a support call at work and start worrying I’m going to make some mistake, or that I missed something obvious, even if nothing has actually gone wrong.\n\nAnd then it can turn into this bigger, vague fear about my life overall—like how am I ever going to save money, how am I going to move forward, what if I’m stuck like this. So yes, even when there isn’t one clear thing happening right then, I still feel anxious. It’s like the worry just moves around and attaches itself to whatever it can find.\n\n[Last Question]\nIf the condo foreclosure had never happened, do you think you would still be feeling this anxious?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / computer_support_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"My worry doesn't stay on the condo anymore; it jumps from whether my cousin is upset with me, to whether I'll make a mistake on a support call, to how I'll ever save money or move forward with my life.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… yes, probably. I mean, the condo situation definitely made everything louder and harder to ignore, so I don’t want to act like it didn’t matter. But when I really think about it, the worrying doesn’t stay neatly tied to the condo anymore.

It jumps around. Like one minute I’m worrying about the condo, and then suddenly I’m wondering if my cousin is upset with me because of some tiny change in how she texted. Or I’ll be on a support call at work and start thinking, “What if I mess this up? What if I miss something obvious?” even when there isn’t actually a clear problem happening.

And then it can get bigger, like I start worrying about how I’m ever going to save money, or move forward, or get my life feeling stable again. So… yeah. I think even if the foreclosure hadn’t happened, I’d still be anxious. Maybe not in exactly the same way, or maybe not as intensely, but I don’t think all of this is only because of that.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.23, F41.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s nice to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah, nice to meet you too. I came in because I just feel so wound up almost all the time. Like my body is constantly bracing for something bad to happen, even when there’s no actual reason for it. I’ll be sitting at my desk at work, doing something completely ordinary, and I can feel myself all tense, like I’m waiting for the next problem or disaster or something.\n\nAnd it’s not just at work. Even when I’m trying to do something that’s supposed to be fun, like dance night, I still feel that same tight, keyed-up feeling. It’s like I can’t really settle into the moment because my body is already preparing for the next bad thing. It’s exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you before these feelings started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of one big traumatic event that I can point to and say, “That’s when this started.” I’ve tried to think about that, honestly, because it would almost be easier if there were a clear before-and-after, but I can’t really come up with anything like that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had stressful periods like anyone, and things that were upsetting or overwhelming at the time, but nothing that feels like a major trauma right before this began. It’s more like the feelings kind of built up or became more noticeable over time, if that makes sense. I’m not saying nothing ever affected me, but there isn’t one specific event that stands out as the cause.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get sudden surges of intense fear or physical panic that peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I get what I would call these sudden, out-of-nowhere attacks where it spikes really fast and then peaks within a few minutes. At least, I haven’t noticed it in that clear-cut way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m already tense and worried, and it kind of builds or just stays there. But when you say “physical panic,” I guess I’m not sure exactly what counts. Like, I do get really uncomfortable in my body sometimes, but I don’t know that it’s this sudden surge of intense fear that comes on all at once. It feels less like a wave crashing and more like… this ongoing pressure that I’m carrying around.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific stressful life change or event going on around the time these feelings started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there was. It’s a little hard for me to separate everything out, but the timing lines up with when the condo loan started moving toward foreclosure. That was about nine months ago.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just a stressful financial/legal kind of situation and that I was reacting normally, but then the feeling just didn’t really shut off. Like once that process started, it was as if my brain decided everything was unstable and dangerous, and I’ve kind of been stuck in that mode since then. So… yes, I’d say around nine months, connected to the condo loan situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about several different parts of your life, not just the condo situation?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. I think at first it was mostly the condo thing, or at least that was the thing I could point to and say, “Okay, this is why I’m stressed.” But now it doesn’t stay there at all. It’s like my mind uses that as a starting point and then just jumps to everything else.\n\nLike I’ll worry about whether my cousin is upset with me because she texted a little differently than usual, and then I’ll replay what I said and wonder if I sounded rude or needy or something. Then at work, I’ll worry I’m going to make some stupid mistake on a support call, even if it’s something I technically know how to handle. I’ll be thinking ahead like, what if I misunderstand the issue, what if I give the wrong answer, what if someone complains.\n\nAnd then it goes bigger, too, like how I’m ever going to save money or move forward with my life. It’s not just one problem anymore. It feels like my brain keeps scanning for things that could go wrong, and there’s always something available.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been feeling this anxious and on edge most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I’d say about nine months now. It really started around when the condo loan situation began moving toward foreclosure. Before that I mean, I’d get stressed like anyone, but this ongoing anxious, on-edge feeling… that’s been since then.\n\nIt’s weird because at first I thought, okay, this is just because of the foreclosure stuff, and once I understand what’s happening or once something gets resolved, I’ll calm down. But it didn’t really work that way. It’s been kind of consistently there since then, like in the background most days. So… yeah, about nine months.\n\nDoctor: If the condo foreclosure situation were fully resolved, do you think your anxiety would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would fully go away, honestly. I wish I could say yes, because that would make it feel simpler, like there’s one main problem and once it’s fixed my brain will calm down. And I do think resolving the condo situation would help a lot — like it would take away this huge pressure that’s been hanging over me.\n\nBut the worry doesn’t really stay there anymore. It kind of spreads. I’ll start with something related to the condo, and then suddenly I’m thinking, what if my cousin is mad at me because her text sounded a little short, or I’ll replay something I said and wonder if I came off wrong. Then at work I’ll be on a support call and worry I’m going to make a mistake or misunderstand something, even if I know what I’m doing. And then it turns into bigger stuff, like how I’m supposed to save money or move forward with my life at all.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think it would mostly disappear. I think one big trigger would be gone, but my mind would probably just find the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Has this anxiety been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve started falling behind on support tickets because I keep checking and re-checking things that should be simple. Like, I’ll write a response to someone or document a fix, and instead of just sending it or closing the ticket, I’ll read it over again and again because I’m worried I missed something obvious or phrased something wrong. And then something that should take a few minutes turns into this whole drawn-out thing.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where coworkers have had to pick up the slack sometimes, which feels awful, because I don’t want to be that person. I know they’re not necessarily mad at me, but I feel embarrassed, like I’m not carrying my weight.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped several dance nights and mentoring sessions. Those are things I normally care about and actually enjoy, but lately I’ll feel so overwhelmed before going that I just don’t show up. I tell myself I’ll go next time, or that I’m too drained, but then afterward I feel guilty and kind of disappointed in myself. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety has been stronger than you’d expect for the condo foreclosure situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has been stronger than I’d expect, even though the foreclosure situation is genuinely stressful. I don’t want to act like that’s a small thing, because it’s not. But the level of worry feels like it’s taken on a life of its own.\n\nAt first it made sense to me that I was anxious about the condo, because that was the obvious problem. But now it doesn’t stay there. It jumps around. I’ll be worrying about the condo, and then suddenly I’m wondering if my cousin is upset with me because she answered a text kind of briefly, and I’ll replay the whole exchange in my head. Or I’ll be at work on a support call and start worrying that I’m going to make a mistake or miss something, even when I’ve handled similar things plenty of times before. And then it turns into this bigger spiral about how I’m ever going to save money or get my life moving in the right direction.\n\nSo… yes, I think it’s more than what the foreclosure alone explains. It feels like my brain is treating everything like a possible disaster now, not just that one situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the most frustrating parts, because I can recognize, logically, that I’ve already done the thing I’m worried about. Like with work, I’ll tell myself, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You already sent the email, it’s fine,” but that doesn’t really stop it. The thought just keeps looping in the background.\n\nAnd then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back. What if I missed a number, what if I misunderstood the request, what if the email sounded too blunt, what if someone notices and thinks I’m careless… it just branches out. I try to reassure myself, but it doesn’t always stick, or it only works for a minute and then I’m right back in it.\n\nSo yes, I do find it hard to control once it starts. I can sometimes distract myself eventually, but it takes a lot more effort than it feels like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, very often. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because I feel wound up almost all the time.\n\nIt’s like my body is bracing for the next bad thing, even when nothing is actively happening. I can be sitting at my desk, just doing normal work stuff, and I’ll notice my shoulders are tight or I’m kind of on edge, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong. And it’s not always dramatic from the outside—I’m not pacing around constantly or anything—but internally I feel keyed up, like I can’t fully settle.\n\nEven at dance nights, which should be fun and something I usually enjoy, I’ll sometimes feel this tension in me, like I’m physically there but my body is still in “watch out” mode. It makes it hard to relax into anything. So yes… restless is a good word for it, but it also feels like being tense and wound tight all the time.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the condo foreclosure, do you feel anxious even when nothing in particular seems wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s the part that’s been confusing to me, because I keep trying to tell myself, “Okay, the condo foreclosure is the reason you’re stressed,” and that would make sense if the worry stayed there. But it doesn’t. It’s like once my mind gets going, it just looks for the next possible problem.\n\nI’ll be thinking about the condo, and then suddenly I’m worrying that my cousin is upset with me because she sent a short text or didn’t use an emoji or something tiny like that. So yes, even when there isn’t one clear thing happening right then, I still feel anxious. It’s like the worry just moves around and attaches itself to whatever it can find.\n\nDoctor: If the condo foreclosure had never happened, do you think you would still be feeling this anxious?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. But when I really think about it, the worrying doesn’t stay neatly tied to the condo anymore.\n\nIt jumps around. Not mainly, no. I mean, the condo foreclosure is definitely a huge stressor, and I’d be lying if I said it wasn’t a big part of what I think about. But the anxiety doesn’t really stay there anymore.

It’s like my mind starts with the condo, and then it branches out into everything else. I’ll suddenly start wondering if my cousin is upset with me because she sounded a little short in a text. Or I’ll be on a support call at work and worry I’m going to make some obvious mistake, even if there’s no real sign that I am. And then it turns into bigger life stuff, like, how am I supposed to save money, or move forward, or get my life back on track.

So I guess the condo is one major thing, but it’s not the only thing. The worry kind of jumps around and attaches itself to whatever feels uncertain.

Then at work, I’ll worry I’m going to make some stupid mistake on a support call, even if it’s something I technically know how to handle. I’ll be thinking ahead like, what if I misunderstand the issue, what if I give the wrong answer, what if someone complains.\n\nAnd then it goes bigger, too, like how I’m ever going to save money or move forward with my life. It feels like my brain keeps scanning for things that could go wrong, and there’s always something available.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been feeling this anxious and on edge most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I’d say about nine months now. It really started around when the condo loan situation began moving toward foreclosure. Before that I mean, I’d get stressed like anyone, but this ongoing anxious, on-edge feeling… that’s been since then.\n\nIt’s weird because at first I thought, okay, this is just because of the foreclosure stuff, and once I understand what’s happening or once something gets resolved, I’ll calm down. But it didn’t really work that way. It’s been kind of consistently there since then, like in the background most days. So… yeah, about nine months.\n\nDoctor: If the condo foreclosure situation were fully resolved, do you think your anxiety would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would fully go away, honestly. I wish I could say yes, because that would make it feel simpler, like there’s one main problem and once it’s fixed my brain will calm down. And I do think resolving the condo situation would help a lot — like it would take away this huge pressure that’s been hanging over me.\n\nBut the worry doesn’t really stay there anymore. It kind of spreads. I’ll start with something related to the condo, and then suddenly I’m thinking, what if my cousin is mad at me because her text sounded a little short, or I’ll replay something I said and wonder if I came off wrong. Then at work I’ll be on a support call and worry I’m going to make a mistake or misunderstand something, even if I know what I’m doing. And then it turns into bigger stuff, like how I’m supposed to save money or move forward with my life at all.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think it would mostly disappear. I think one big trigger would be gone, but my mind would probably just find the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Has this anxiety been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve started falling behind on support tickets because I keep checking and re-checking things that should be simple. Like, I’ll write a response to someone or document a fix, and instead of just sending it or closing the ticket, I’ll read it over again and again because I’m worried I missed something obvious or phrased something wrong. And then something that should take a few minutes turns into this whole drawn-out thing.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where coworkers have had to pick up the slack sometimes, which feels awful, because I don’t want to be that person. I know they’re not necessarily mad at me, but I feel embarrassed, like I’m not carrying my weight.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped several dance nights and mentoring sessions. Those are things I normally care about and actually enjoy, but lately I’ll feel so overwhelmed before going that I just don’t show up. I tell myself I’ll go next time, or that I’m too drained, but then afterward I feel guilty and kind of disappointed in myself. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety has been stronger than you’d expect for the condo foreclosure situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has been stronger than I’d expect, even though the foreclosure situation is genuinely stressful. I don’t want to act like that’s a small thing, because it’s not. But the level of worry feels like it’s taken on a life of its own.\n\nAt first it made sense to me that I was anxious about the condo, because that was the obvious problem. I’ll be worrying about the condo, and then suddenly I’m wondering if my cousin is upset with me because she answered a text kind of briefly, and I’ll replay the whole exchange in my head. Or I’ll be at work on a support call and start worrying that I’m going to make a mistake or miss something, even when I’ve handled similar things plenty of times before. And then it turns into this bigger spiral about how I’m ever going to save money or get my life moving in the right direction.\n\nSo… yes, I think it’s more than what the foreclosure alone explains. It feels like my brain is treating everything like a possible disaster now, not just that one situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the most frustrating parts, because I can recognize, logically, that I’ve already done the thing I’m worried about. Like with work, I’ll tell myself, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You already sent the email, it’s fine,” but that doesn’t really stop it. The thought just keeps looping in the background.\n\nAnd then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back. What if I missed a number, what if I misunderstood the request, what if the email sounded too blunt, what if someone notices and thinks I’m careless… it just branches out. I try to reassure myself, but it doesn’t always stick, or it only works for a minute and then I’m right back in it.\n\nSo yes, I do find it hard to control once it starts. I can sometimes distract myself eventually, but it takes a lot more effort than it feels like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, very often. I can be sitting at my desk, just doing normal work stuff, and I’ll notice my shoulders are tight or I’m kind of on edge, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong. And it’s not always dramatic from the outside—I’m not pacing around constantly or anything—but internally I feel keyed up, like I can’t fully settle.\n\nEven at dance nights, which should be fun and something I usually enjoy, I’ll sometimes feel this tension in me, like I’m physically there but my body is still in “watch out” mode. It makes it hard to relax into anything. But it doesn’t. So yes, even when there isn’t one clear thing happening right then, I still feel anxious. I’m not saying nothing ever affected me, but there isn’t one specific event that stands out as the cause.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get sudden surges of intense fear or physical panic that peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I get what I would call these sudden, out-of-nowhere attacks where it spikes really fast and then peaks within a few minutes. At least, I haven’t noticed it in that clear-cut way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m already tense and worried, and it kind of builds or just stays there. But when you say “physical panic,” I guess I’m not sure exactly what counts. Like, I do get really uncomfortable in my body sometimes, but I don’t know that it’s this sudden surge of intense fear that comes on all at once. It feels less like a wave crashing and more like… this ongoing pressure that I’m carrying around.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific stressful life change or event going on around the time these feelings started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there was. It’s a little hard for me to separate everything out, but the timing lines up with when the condo loan started moving toward foreclosure. That was about nine months ago.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just a stressful financial/legal kind of situation and that I was reacting normally, but then the feeling just didn’t really shut off. Like once that process started, it was as if my brain decided everything was unstable and dangerous, and I’ve kind of been stuck in that mode since then. So… yes, I’d say around nine months, connected to the condo loan situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about several different parts of your life, not just the condo situation?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. I think at first it was mostly the condo thing, or at least that was the thing I could point to and say, “Okay, this is why I’m stressed.” But now it doesn’t stay there at all. It’s like my mind uses that as a starting point and then just jumps to everything else.\n\nLike I’ll worry about whether my cousin is upset with me because she texted a little differently than usual, and then I’ll replay what I said and wonder if I sounded rude or needy or something. Then at work, I’ll worry I’m going to make some stupid mistake on a support call, even if it’s something I technically know how to handle. I’ll be thinking ahead like, what if I misunderstand the issue, what if I give the wrong answer, what if someone complains.\n\nAnd then it goes bigger, too, like how I’m ever going to save money or move forward with my life. It’s not just one problem anymore. It feels like my brain keeps scanning for things that could go wrong, and there’s always something available.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been feeling this anxious and on edge most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I’d say about nine months now. It really started around when the condo loan situation began moving toward foreclosure. Before that I mean, I’d get stressed like anyone, but this ongoing anxious, on-edge feeling… that’s been since then.\n\nIt’s weird because at first I thought, okay, this is just because of the foreclosure stuff, and once I understand what’s happening or once something gets resolved, I’ll calm down. But it didn’t really work that way. It’s been kind of consistently there since then, like in the background most days. So… yeah, about nine months.\n\nDoctor: If the condo foreclosure situation were fully resolved, do you think your anxiety would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would fully go away, honestly. I wish I could say yes, because that would make it feel simpler, like there’s one main problem and once it’s fixed my brain will calm down. And I do think resolving the condo situation would help a lot — like it would take away this huge pressure that’s been hanging over me.\n\nBut the worry doesn’t really stay there anymore. It kind of spreads. I’ll start with something related to the condo, and then suddenly I’m thinking, what if my cousin is mad at me because her text sounded a little short, or I’ll replay something I said and wonder if I came off wrong. Then at work I’ll be on a support call and worry I’m going to make a mistake or misunderstand something, even if I know what I’m doing. And then it turns into bigger stuff, like how I’m supposed to save money or move forward with my life at all.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think it would mostly disappear. I think one big trigger would be gone, but my mind would probably just find the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Has this anxiety been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve started falling behind on support tickets because I keep checking and re-checking things that should be simple. Like, I’ll write a response to someone or document a fix, and instead of just sending it or closing the ticket, I’ll read it over again and again because I’m worried I missed something obvious or phrased something wrong. And then something that should take a few minutes turns into this whole drawn-out thing.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where coworkers have had to pick up the slack sometimes, which feels awful, because I don’t want to be that person. I know they’re not necessarily mad at me, but I feel embarrassed, like I’m not carrying my weight.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped several dance nights and mentoring sessions. Those are things I normally care about and actually enjoy, but lately I’ll feel so overwhelmed before going that I just don’t show up. I tell myself I’ll go next time, or that I’m too drained, but then afterward I feel guilty and kind of disappointed in myself. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety has been stronger than you’d expect for the condo foreclosure situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has been stronger than I’d expect, even though the foreclosure situation is genuinely stressful. I don’t want to act like that’s a small thing, because it’s not. But the level of worry feels like it’s taken on a life of its own.\n\nAt first it made sense to me that I was anxious about the condo, because that was the obvious problem. But now it doesn’t stay there. It jumps around. I’ll be worrying about the condo, and then suddenly I’m wondering if my cousin is upset with me because she answered a text kind of briefly, and I’ll replay the whole exchange in my head. Or I’ll be at work on a support call and start worrying that I’m going to make a mistake or miss something, even when I’ve handled similar things plenty of times before. And then it turns into this bigger spiral about how I’m ever going to save money or get my life moving in the right direction.\n\nSo… yes, I think it’s more than what the foreclosure alone explains. It feels like my brain is treating everything like a possible disaster now, not just that one situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the most frustrating parts, because I can recognize, logically, that I’ve already done the thing I’m worried about. Like with work, I’ll tell myself, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You already sent the email, it’s fine,” but that doesn’t really stop it. The thought just keeps looping in the background.\n\nAnd then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back. What if I missed a number, what if I misunderstood the request, what if the email sounded too blunt, what if someone notices and thinks I’m careless… it just branches out. I try to reassure myself, but it doesn’t always stick, or it only works for a minute and then I’m right back in it.\n\nSo yes, I do find it hard to control once it starts. I can sometimes distract myself eventually, but it takes a lot more effort than it feels like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, very often. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because I feel wound up almost all the time.\n\nIt’s like my body is bracing for the next bad thing, even when nothing is actively happening. I can be sitting at my desk, just doing normal work stuff, and I’ll notice my shoulders are tight or I’m kind of on edge, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong. And it’s not always dramatic from the outside—I’m not pacing around constantly or anything—but internally I feel keyed up, like I can’t fully settle.\n\nEven at dance nights, which should be fun and something I usually enjoy, I’ll sometimes feel this tension in me, like I’m physically there but my body is still in “watch out” mode. It makes it hard to relax into anything. So yes… restless is a good word for it, but it also feels like being tense and wound tight all the time.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the condo foreclosure, do you feel anxious even when nothing in particular seems wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s the part that’s been confusing to me, because I keep trying to tell myself, “Okay, the condo foreclosure is the reason you’re stressed,” and that would make sense if the worry stayed there. But it doesn’t. It’s like once my mind gets going, it just looks for the next possible problem.\n\nI’ll be thinking about the condo, and then suddenly I’m worrying that my cousin is upset with me because she sent a short text or didn’t use an emoji or something tiny like that. And I know how that sounds, like I’m over-reading it, but in the moment it feels really hard not to analyze it. Then later I’ll be on a support call at work and start worrying I’m going to make some mistake, or that I missed something obvious, even if nothing has actually gone wrong.\n\nAnd then it can turn into this bigger, vague fear about my life overall—like how am I ever going to save money, how am I going to move forward, what if I’m stuck like this. So yes, even when there isn’t one clear thing happening right then, I still feel anxious. It’s like the worry just moves around and attaches itself to whatever it can find.\n\nDoctor: If the condo foreclosure had never happened, do you think you would still be feeling this anxious?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, the condo situation definitely made everything louder and harder to ignore, so I don’t want to act like it didn’t matter. But when I really think about it, the worrying doesn’t stay neatly tied to the condo anymore.\n\nIt jumps around. Like one minute I’m worrying about the condo, and then suddenly I’m wondering if my cousin is upset with me because of some tiny change in how she texted. Or I’ll be on a support call at work and start thinking, “What if I mess this up? What if I miss something obvious?” even when there isn’t actually a clear problem happening.\n\nAnd then it can get bigger, like I start worrying about how I’m ever going to save money, or move forward, or get my life feeling stable again. So… yeah. I think even if the foreclosure hadn’t happened, I’d still be anxious. Maybe not in exactly the same way, or maybe not as intensely, but I don’t think all of this is only because of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is mainly tied to the condo foreclosure situation?\n\nPatient: Not mainly, no. I mean, the condo foreclosure is definitely a huge stressor, and I’d be lying if I said it wasn’t a big part of what I think about. But the anxiety doesn’t really stay there anymore.\n\nIt’s like my mind starts with the condo, and then it branches out into everything else. I’ll suddenly start wondering if my cousin is upset with me because she sounded a little short in a text. Or I’ll be on a support call at work and worry I’m going to make some obvious mistake, even if there’s no real sign that I am. And then it turns into bigger life stuff, like, how am I supposed to save money, or move forward, or get my life back on track.\n\nSo I guess the condo is one major thing, but it’s not the only thing. The worry kind of jumps around and attaches itself to whatever feels uncertain.\n\nDoctor: Do you get tired easily because of this anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how much of my tiredness is from the anxiety versus just, you know, normal life and work. I do feel worn down sometimes, especially after I’ve been worrying for a while, but I haven’t really tracked it closely enough to say, “Yes, this is definitely because of anxiety.”\n\nIt’s more like I’ll notice that I feel kind of drained after my mind has been going in circles, but I don’t know if I’d say I get tired easily in a clear, consistent way. I guess I’d say I’ve noticed some exhaustion, but I’m not totally sure what to attribute it to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. That’s actually one of the more noticeable things for me, especially at work.

Like, I’ll be writing a script or trying to troubleshoot something, and I’ll know what I’m supposed to be doing, but then my mind just… slips off track. I’ll start thinking about everything that could go wrong, or whether I’m going to miss something important, and then suddenly I’m just staring at the screen. And it’s not like I’m choosing to daydream. It feels more like my brain gets pulled away.

It can happen when I’m helping someone too, whether I’m speaking English or Spanish. I’ll be listening to the person explain the issue, and then some anxious thought pops in, and I lose the thread of what they were saying for a second. Then I have to kind of scramble to catch back up or ask them to repeat something, which of course makes me feel more aware that I’m not concentrating.

So yes, I’d say the anxiety definitely interferes with my concentration. Not every single minute, but often enough that I notice it and it affects how confident I feel doing my job.

It’s exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Did something traumatic happen to you before these feelings started?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of one big traumatic event that I can point to and say, “That’s when this started.” I’ve tried to think about that, honestly, because it would almost be easier if there were a clear before-and-after, but I can’t really come up with anything like that.\n\nI mean, I’ve had stressful periods like anyone, and things that were upsetting or overwhelming at the time, but nothing that feels like a major trauma right before this began. It’s more like the feelings kind of built up or became more noticeable over time, if that makes sense. I’m not saying nothing ever affected me, but there isn’t one specific event that stands out as the cause.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get sudden surges of intense fear or physical panic that peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I get what I would call these sudden, out-of-nowhere attacks where it spikes really fast and then peaks within a few minutes. At least, I haven’t noticed it in that clear-cut way.\n\nIt’s more like I’m already tense and worried, and it kind of builds or just stays there. But when you say “physical panic,” I guess I’m not sure exactly what counts. Like, I do get really uncomfortable in my body sometimes, but I don’t know that it’s this sudden surge of intense fear that comes on all at once. It feels less like a wave crashing and more like… this ongoing pressure that I’m carrying around.\n\nDoctor: Was there a specific stressful life change or event going on around the time these feelings started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think there was. It’s a little hard for me to separate everything out, but the timing lines up with when the condo loan started moving toward foreclosure. That was about nine months ago.\n\nAt first I kept telling myself it was just a stressful financial/legal kind of situation and that I was reacting normally, but then the feeling just didn’t really shut off. Like once that process started, it was as if my brain decided everything was unstable and dangerous, and I’ve kind of been stuck in that mode since then. So… yes, I’d say around nine months, connected to the condo loan situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about several different parts of your life, not just the condo situation?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. I think at first it was mostly the condo thing, or at least that was the thing I could point to and say, “Okay, this is why I’m stressed.” But now it doesn’t stay there at all. It’s like my mind uses that as a starting point and then just jumps to everything else.\n\nLike I’ll worry about whether my cousin is upset with me because she texted a little differently than usual, and then I’ll replay what I said and wonder if I sounded rude or needy or something. Then at work, I’ll worry I’m going to make some stupid mistake on a support call, even if it’s something I technically know how to handle. I’ll be thinking ahead like, what if I misunderstand the issue, what if I give the wrong answer, what if someone complains.\n\nAnd then it goes bigger, too, like how I’m ever going to save money or move forward with my life. It’s not just one problem anymore. It feels like my brain keeps scanning for things that could go wrong, and there’s always something available.\n\nDoctor: For how long have you been feeling this anxious and on edge most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I’d say about nine months now. It really started around when the condo loan situation began moving toward foreclosure. Before that I mean, I’d get stressed like anyone, but this ongoing anxious, on-edge feeling… that’s been since then.\n\nIt’s weird because at first I thought, okay, this is just because of the foreclosure stuff, and once I understand what’s happening or once something gets resolved, I’ll calm down. But it didn’t really work that way. It’s been kind of consistently there since then, like in the background most days. So… yeah, about nine months.\n\nDoctor: If the condo foreclosure situation were fully resolved, do you think your anxiety would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it would fully go away, honestly. I wish I could say yes, because that would make it feel simpler, like there’s one main problem and once it’s fixed my brain will calm down. And I do think resolving the condo situation would help a lot — like it would take away this huge pressure that’s been hanging over me.\n\nBut the worry doesn’t really stay there anymore. It kind of spreads. I’ll start with something related to the condo, and then suddenly I’m thinking, what if my cousin is mad at me because her text sounded a little short, or I’ll replay something I said and wonder if I came off wrong. Then at work I’ll be on a support call and worry I’m going to make a mistake or misunderstand something, even if I know what I’m doing. And then it turns into bigger stuff, like how I’m supposed to save money or move forward with my life at all.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think it would mostly disappear. I think one big trigger would be gone, but my mind would probably just find the next thing to latch onto.\n\nDoctor: Has this anxiety been interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has. More than I wanted to admit at first.\n\nAt work especially, I’ve started falling behind on support tickets because I keep checking and re-checking things that should be simple. Like, I’ll write a response to someone or document a fix, and instead of just sending it or closing the ticket, I’ll read it over again and again because I’m worried I missed something obvious or phrased something wrong. And then something that should take a few minutes turns into this whole drawn-out thing.\n\nIt’s gotten to the point where coworkers have had to pick up the slack sometimes, which feels awful, because I don’t want to be that person. I know they’re not necessarily mad at me, but I feel embarrassed, like I’m not carrying my weight.\n\nAnd outside of work, I’ve skipped several dance nights and mentoring sessions. Those are things I normally care about and actually enjoy, but lately I’ll feel so overwhelmed before going that I just don’t show up. I tell myself I’ll go next time, or that I’m too drained, but then afterward I feel guilty and kind of disappointed in myself. So yes, it’s definitely affecting my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety has been stronger than you’d expect for the condo foreclosure situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has been stronger than I’d expect, even though the foreclosure situation is genuinely stressful. I don’t want to act like that’s a small thing, because it’s not. But the level of worry feels like it’s taken on a life of its own.\n\nAt first it made sense to me that I was anxious about the condo, because that was the obvious problem. But now it doesn’t stay there. It jumps around. I’ll be worrying about the condo, and then suddenly I’m wondering if my cousin is upset with me because she answered a text kind of briefly, and I’ll replay the whole exchange in my head. Or I’ll be at work on a support call and start worrying that I’m going to make a mistake or miss something, even when I’ve handled similar things plenty of times before. And then it turns into this bigger spiral about how I’m ever going to save money or get my life moving in the right direction.\n\nSo… yes, I think it’s more than what the foreclosure alone explains. It feels like my brain is treating everything like a possible disaster now, not just that one situation.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control or stop the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually one of the most frustrating parts, because I can recognize, logically, that I’ve already done the thing I’m worried about. Like with work, I’ll tell myself, “You already checked the spreadsheet,” or “You already sent the email, it’s fine,” but that doesn’t really stop it. The thought just keeps looping in the background.\n\nAnd then it’s like one “what if” turns into five more before I can pull myself back. What if I missed a number, what if I misunderstood the request, what if the email sounded too blunt, what if someone notices and thinks I’m careless… it just branches out. I try to reassure myself, but it doesn’t always stick, or it only works for a minute and then I’m right back in it.\n\nSo yes, I do find it hard to control once it starts. I can sometimes distract myself eventually, but it takes a lot more effort than it feels like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless?\n\nPatient: Yes, very often. That’s actually one of the main reasons I came in, because I feel wound up almost all the time.\n\nIt’s like my body is bracing for the next bad thing, even when nothing is actively happening. I can be sitting at my desk, just doing normal work stuff, and I’ll notice my shoulders are tight or I’m kind of on edge, like I’m waiting for something to go wrong. And it’s not always dramatic from the outside—I’m not pacing around constantly or anything—but internally I feel keyed up, like I can’t fully settle.\n\nEven at dance nights, which should be fun and something I usually enjoy, I’ll sometimes feel this tension in me, like I’m physically there but my body is still in “watch out” mode. It makes it hard to relax into anything. So yes… restless is a good word for it, but it also feels like being tense and wound tight all the time.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the condo foreclosure, do you feel anxious even when nothing in particular seems wrong?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s the part that’s been confusing to me, because I keep trying to tell myself, “Okay, the condo foreclosure is the reason you’re stressed,” and that would make sense if the worry stayed there. But it doesn’t. It’s like once my mind gets going, it just looks for the next possible problem.\n\nI’ll be thinking about the condo, and then suddenly I’m worrying that my cousin is upset with me because she sent a short text or didn’t use an emoji or something tiny like that. And I know how that sounds, like I’m over-reading it, but in the moment it feels really hard not to analyze it. Then later I’ll be on a support call at work and start worrying I’m going to make some mistake, or that I missed something obvious, even if nothing has actually gone wrong.\n\nAnd then it can turn into this bigger, vague fear about my life overall—like how am I ever going to save money, how am I going to move forward, what if I’m stuck like this. So yes, even when there isn’t one clear thing happening right then, I still feel anxious. It’s like the worry just moves around and attaches itself to whatever it can find.\n\nDoctor: If the condo foreclosure had never happened, do you think you would still be feeling this anxious?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. I mean, the condo situation definitely made everything louder and harder to ignore, so I don’t want to act like it didn’t matter. But when I really think about it, the worrying doesn’t stay neatly tied to the condo anymore.\n\nIt jumps around. Like one minute I’m worrying about the condo, and then suddenly I’m wondering if my cousin is upset with me because of some tiny change in how she texted. Or I’ll be on a support call at work and start thinking, “What if I mess this up? What if I miss something obvious?” even when there isn’t actually a clear problem happening.\n\nAnd then it can get bigger, like I start worrying about how I’m ever going to save money, or move forward, or get my life feeling stable again. So… yeah. I think even if the foreclosure hadn’t happened, I’d still be anxious. Maybe not in exactly the same way, or maybe not as intensely, but I don’t think all of this is only because of that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your anxiety is mainly tied to the condo foreclosure situation?\n\nPatient: Not mainly, no. I’ll suddenly start wondering if my cousin is upset with me because she sounded a little short in a text. The worry kind of jumps around and attaches itself to whatever feels uncertain.\n\nDoctor: Do you get tired easily because of this anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how much of my tiredness is from the anxiety versus just, you know, normal life and work. I guess I’d say I’ve noticed some exhaustion, but I’m not totally sure what to attribute it to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble concentrating because of the anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. And it’s not like I’m choosing to daydream. It feels more like my brain gets pulled away.\n\nIt can happen when I’m helping someone too, whether I’m speaking English or Spanish. I’ll be listening to the person explain the issue, and then some anxious thought pops in, and I lose the thread of what they were saying for a second. Then I have to kind of scramble to catch back up or ask them to repeat something, which of course makes me feel more aware that I’m not concentrating.\n\nSo yes, I’d say the anxiety definitely interferes with my concentration.
